生産者物価も大幅上昇し、インフレへの警戒強まる

日本：7月以降の設備投資需要はAI関連産業を中心に旺盛
3
 7～9月期の機械受注は増勢回復へ。AI関連業種からの受注がけん引
― ①旺盛なAI需要のもと、関連業種では設備不足感が強まっていたこと、②輸出増が持続するもとで、好業績が期待され
ること、③中東情勢の不確実性が後退したことなどが背景
 先行き設備投資は緩やかな増勢を辿る見込み
― 半導体製造装置をはじめ、AI・データセンター関連品目を中心に受注残が増加しており、目先こうした傾向が続く見通し
― 積み上がった受注残を段階的に消化することで、設備投資が持続的に下支えされる公算が大きい

世界：AI関連投資の拡大が鉱物需要を押し上げる一方、鈍い資源開発投資の回復ペース
4
 AI関連需要の拡大が銅やレアアースの需給環境に影響
― データセンター建設や電力インフラ整備に伴い、銅やレアアースの需要が拡大。ガリウム等の戦略鉱物への需要も増加
 資源開発投資の回復が遅れるなか、中長期的な供給不足への懸念強まる
―IEAは、2035年に銅が供給量対比で約26％の供給不足に陥ると予測。レアアースは供給不足を回避する見込みながら、

採掘・精錬において中国が支配的地位を占めており、供給元の集中がリスク
IEA：銅、レアアースの需要見通し IEA：銅・レアアースの需給バランス予測（2035年）
（出所）IEA "Global Critical Minerals Outlook 2026"より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
AI関連投資の拡大を背景に、
銅やレアアースの需要が拡大
銅は供給が不足へ
レアアースは供給超過も供給元の集中がリスク
注目点①AI投資拡大と資源供給制約

世界：AI投資が金融市場を下支えする一方、持続性への懸念は残存
 IMFはAI投資や企業収益の拡大を背景に金融市場が底堅さを維持していると評価
― ただし、金融安定リスクは引き続き高いとの評価で、AI、国債市場、サイバーを主要なリスク要因として指摘
 AIについてはAI主導の市場調整リスクを警戒
―AI中核企業の収益拡大によって株価の割高感は緩和する一方、社債発行や循環的な資金調達が拡大。投資収益が期
待を下回った場合、金融市場への影響が増幅される可能性
5

世界：国債市場に残る脆弱性。インフレ・財政懸念による金利急騰リスクは増大
 主要国の長期金利は、期待短期金利とタームプレミアムの双方から上昇
―IMFは財政懸念などを背景にタームプレミアムが歴史的に高い領域にあると指摘
― 国債発行の増加や借換リスクの高まりに加え、価格感応度の高い投資家の存在がインフレ・財政ショックによる金利上
昇を増幅する可能性
― 国債市場は足元で秩序を維持するも、急激な金利上昇は資金調達市場の逼迫や銀行部門への波及を招くリスク
6

### Page 8

世界：ヘッジファンドのレバレッジ拡大が市場ショックを増幅、銀行部門への波及にも警戒
 高まるヘッジファンドの存在感と市場への影響力
― ヘッジファンドの総資産は2013年の約4兆ドルから2026年初には約13兆ドルに拡大。平時には市場の流動性や効率性
を支える一方、ストレス時にはレバレッジ解消や取引集中が価格変動を増幅させる懸念
 ヘッジファンドの損失がプライムブローカーを通じて銀行部門に波及するリスク
―IMFはヘッジファンドの運用成績悪化がプライムブローカーの収益にも悪影響を及ぼすと分析。銀行自身の健全性に加
え、ノンバンクとの相互連関を通じた金融システム全体への波及にも警鐘
7

世界：2026年の世界経済は底堅く推移も、経済成長の恩恵やインフレの負担は一部に偏在
 IMFは2026年の世界成長率を小幅に上方修正（3.0％→3.1％）
―AIブームを追い風とした半導体輸出の増加等を背景に、アジアを中心に上方修正
― 備蓄放出・代替調達等による政府・企業の対応が原油供給ショックによる下押しを緩和したことも影響
 一方、AI需要の取り込みや供給ショックへの耐性には国家間で格差
―IMFによれば、世界の経済成長の95％を占める国の数は54（4月）から32（10月）に減少したほか、今次改定では47％
の国が成長率を下方修正。AIブームの恩恵や供給ショックによる負担は一部の国・地域に偏在

（年／月）
日本：コメ価格下落で企業物価は鈍化。もっとも、先行きは原油高を背景に再加速へ
18
 9月の国内企業物価はコメ価格の下落を主因に、前年比＋6.8％（前月：同＋7.6％）と鈍化
― 新米価格が昨年を下回ったこと等を背景に、コメ価格が大幅に下落。国内企業物価を▲0.75%Pt押し下げ
― 一方、3月来の原油高を背景に化学・プラスチック製品は高止まり。また、AI需要等を背景に非鉄金属も高い伸びを維持
 7月以降の原油高再燃を背景に、先行きの国内企業物価は再加速する見込み
― 原油価格高止まりにタンカー運賃の急騰が重なり、先行きの輸入物価は原油関連を中心に加速へ
― 国内企業物価は石油・石炭製品やラグを伴いつつ化学・プラスチック製品などを中心に再加速する見込み
（注） コメは玄米及び精米。食品関連は、飲食料品及び農林水産物（除くコメ）

（出所） 日本銀行「企業物価指数」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
国内企業物価 輸入物価（円建て）と国内企業物価
（注） 輸入物価は1カ月先行。原油輸入単価とドバイ原油（1カ月先行）を使用し、輸入物価（契
約通貨建て）の先行きを試算し、為替は1ドル157円程度の推移を想定。二重線部分は見
通し
（出所） 日本銀行「企業物価指数」、財務省「貿易統計」、IMF、LSEGより、みずほ総合研究所

日本

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9 番 1 号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー 
このレポートは投資勧誘を意図して提供するものではありません。このレポートの掲載情報は信頼できると考えられる情報源から作成しておりますが、その正確性、完全

2026 年 10 月 15 日 全9 頁

Indicators Update 
2026 年 8 月機械受注 
製造業・非製造業（船電除く）のいずれも2 カ月ぶりに増加

経済調査部 エコノミスト 吉井 希祐

[要約] 
◼ 2026 年8 月の機械受注（船電除く民需）は前月比＋5.2％と2 カ月ぶりに増加し、コン
センサス（Bloomberg 調査：同＋2.3％）を上回った。民需（船電除く）を 3 カ月移動
平均で見ると同＋3.6％となり、内閣府は機械受注の基調判断を「持ち直しの動きがみ
られる」に据え置いた。 
◼ 製造業からの受注額は前月比＋1.6％と 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。非鉄金属やはん用・生
産用機械、その他輸送用機械などの業種からの受注が増加した。 非製造業（船電除く）
からの受注額は同＋2.9％と 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。リース業や卸売業・小売業、通信
業などの業種からの受注が増加した。 
◼ 先行きの民需（船電除く）は 緩やかな増加基調を辿るとみている。企業の設備投資意
欲は強く、人手不足対応のための省力化投資や、AI 関連投資などが引き続き期待され
よう。ただし、中東情勢のさらなる悪化に伴い 、受注が下振れするリスクには注意が
必要だ。事業環境の不確実性の高まりや原材料の価格高騰・供給不足に対する懸念か
ら、企業の設備投資への姿勢が慎重化する恐れがある。

【総括】船電除く民需は 2 カ月ぶりに増加 
2026 年 8 月の機械受注（船電除く民需）は前月比＋5.2％と 2 カ月ぶりに増加し、コンセン
サス（Bloomberg 調査：同＋2.3％）を上回った（図表2）。製造業は2 カ月ぶりに増加し、非製
造業（船電除く）も2 カ月ぶりに増加した（図表 3）。民需（船電除く）を3 カ月移動平均で見
ると同＋3.6％となり、内閣府は機械受注の基調判断を「持ち直しの動きがみられる」に据え
置いた。

【製造業】非鉄金属などが全体を押し上げたが、業種によりまちまちの結果 
2026 年 8 月の製造業からの受注額は前月比＋1.6％と 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。機種別に見る
と、原動機・重電機、輸送機械、電子・通信機械、工作機械が増加した一方、産業機械は減少
した（図表 4 左、大和総研による季節調整値） 。業種別では、17 業種中 8 業種が増加した。非
鉄金属（同＋132.6％）やはん用・生産用機械（同＋9.3％）、その他輸送用機械（同＋43.7％）
などの業種からの受注が増加した。他方、造船業（同▲ 41.0％）や電気機械（同▲5.9％）な
どからの受注は減少した。化学工業（同▲11.5％）では大型案件が 1 件あったものの、受注増
には至らなかった。

【非製造業】リース業や卸売業・小売業などを中心に増加 
2026 年8 月の非製造業（船電除く）からの受注額は前月比＋2.9％と2 カ月ぶりに増加した。
機種別に見ると、電子・通信機械、原動機・重電機が増加した一方、輸送機械、産業機械、工
作機械は減少した（図表 4 右、大和総研による季節調整値） 。業種別では、11 業種中 7 業種が
増加した。リース業（同＋136.0％）や卸売業・小売業（同＋29.2％）、通信業（同＋16.9％）
などの業種からの受注が増加した。他方、その他非製造業（同▲ 28.0％）や運輸業・郵便業
（同▲13.9％）などからの受注は減少した。

図表 4：業種別・機種別に見た機械受注額の動 き

除外したが、26 年8 月は前月比▲9.5％であった。

【外需】2 カ月連続で減少したものの依然高水準 
2026 年 8 月の外需は前月比▲7.7％と 2 カ月連続で減少した（図表 5） 。機種別では、原動
機・重電機、産業機械、電子・通信機械が減少した一方、工作機械、輸送機械は増加した（ 図
表 6、大和総研による季節調整値） 。外需は 2025 年中旬以降急増していたため、依然高水準で
あるが、増加をけん引してきた電子・通信機械の増勢にはこのところ一服感が見られる。輸送
機械で3 件、電子・通信機械で2 件、原動機・重電機で1 件の合計6 件の大型案件があった。 
機械受注の外需動向を地域別に見る上で参考になる工作機械受注を確認すると、8 月の外需
は前月比＋8.7％と3 カ月連続で増加した（日本工作機械工業会、大和総研による季節調整値、
図表 7） 。米国（同＋15.4％）からの受注は 3 カ月連続で増加した。航空・宇宙関連の大型案件
があったほか、AI 関連の受注が好調だったようだ1。欧州（EU＋英国、同＋16.9％）からの受
注も3 カ月連続で増加した。中国（同＋2.0％）からの受注は2 カ月ぶりに増加した（図表8）。 
工作機械受注は9 月分がすでに公表されており、内需は前月比▲3.3％と4 カ月ぶりに減少し
た。他方、外需は同＋2.8％と4 カ月連続で増加し、過去最高を更新し続けている。

【先行き】民需（船電除く）の緩やかな増加基調を見込むも中東情勢が下振れリスク 
先行きの民需（船電除く）は、緩やかな増加基調を辿るとみている。日銀短観の 9 月調査に
おける「生産・営業用設備判断 DI」 （先行き、全規模）を見ると、製造業（▲6％pt）と非製造
業（▲7％pt）のいずれでも、設備の不足感が示されている（図表 9） 。また、2026 年度の設備
投資計画（全規模、含む土地、ソフトウェアと研究開発投資額は含まない）は、製造業（前年
度比＋8.3％） 、非製造業（同＋7.3％）ともに堅調に推移している（図表 10）。企業の設備投資
意欲は強く、人手不足対応のための省力化投資や、AI 関連投資などが引き続き期待されよう。 
ただし、中東情勢のさらなる悪化に伴い、受注が下振れするリスクには注意が必要だ。ホル
ムズ海峡の通航が困難な状況が続く中、サウジアラビアとイエメンの親イラン武装組織フーシ
の間で攻撃の応酬が続くなど、エネルギー供給をめぐる先行き不透明感は強い。事業環境の不
確実性の高まりや原材料の価格高騰・供給不足に対する懸念から、企業の 設備投資への姿勢が
慎重化する恐れがある。

### Page 1

ab 12 October 2026
Global Research
Nanya Technology
Further upside to expectations likely
Another strong quarter
Nanya Tech (NTC) reported strong 3Q26 revenue of NT$133.6bn (+62% QoQ,
+612% YoY) in line with UBSe of NT$132.7bn and 6% above Visible Alpha consensus
of NT$126.5bn. EPS came in at $25.40, slightly below UBSe of NT$26.44 and modestly
above consensus of NT$24.40. Growth was driven primarily by strong ASPs, up 65%
QoQ (vs UBSe of +50%) mainly on the back of the renewal of 6-month and 1-year

contracts that had expired, partially offset by lower-than-expected bit growth of -
5% QoQ (vs UBSe of +5% QoQ). Profitability, again, improved sequentially, with gross/
operating margins rising to 85.7%/80.0% from 79.5%/73.7% in 2Q26.
Fulfilment rates to remain low until Fab 5A ramp
NTC continues to see supportive supply-demand dynamics, with fulfilment rates for
demand outside of long-term contracts – which account for close to 70% of bits already

– remaining low. That  said, NTC sees limited upside to unit shipments in the near term,
given low inventory levels and limited floor space: (1) reiterating its full-year bit growth
guidance of "up high-teens % YoY," implying 4Q unit shipments will decline MSD
% QoQ (UBSe: -6%); and (2) guiding shipments to remain flat thereafter until its new
fab begins to ramp in 2H27. NTC's efforts to bring Fab 5A online are progressing well,
with equipment move-in scheduled for 1Q27 and initial production expected by 3Q27.

NTC continues to expect wafer capacity to reach 30k wpm by the end of 2028 and 50k
by the end of 2030. With demand remaining strong and existing capacity not enough to
fully address this, NTC continues to see upside to sequential ASP growth in the near
term, although at a more moderate pace.
Changes to forecasts
Reflecting 3Q26 results, as well as our new bit growth and ASP forecasts, we keep our
2026E forecasts largely unchanged but slightly raise our 2027E/2028E revenue forecasts

by 7%/5% to NT$655bn/NT$735bn (from NT$613bn/NT$702bn previously) and
EPS forecasts by 10%/7% to NT$128.16/NT$134.53 (from NT$116.41/NT$125.68
previously). Our 2027E/2028E EPS forecasts now sit 10%/8% above pre-results Visible
Alpha consensus.
Valuation: PT NT$640/share; maintain Buy
We value Nanya Tech shares at 2.63x NTM P/BV based on our 2028E-31E ROE estimate
of 21.4% and CoE of 8.1%.

### Page 2

Nanya Technology UBS ResearchNanya Tech (2408.TW)
Thesis Map     UBS                    Research     THESIS MAP a guide to our thinking and what´s where in this report
Q: Will DRAM undersupply continue into 2028?
Yes, in our view. Agentic AI is boosting further memory demand on multiple fronts beyond HBM,
including DDR5/LPDDR5 for conventional servers and CPU head nodes in AI servers. We now forecast
an acceleration of memory demand growth into 2027 with DRAM bit end-consumption growing

40% YoY vs 21% in 2026. As almost all of the incremental wafer capacity addition for DRAM is
going to HBM, supply is not catching up with demand. The main risk remains affordability by
customers as memory industry revenues are expected to reach US$1.69tn in 2027E.
Q: Can NTC grow as a niche DRAM player?
Yes, we think 2027 is likely to be a year of more constrained growth. Subsequently, though, we think
its process tech migration to 1c nm and 1d nm will likely lead to a 30% increase in bits per wafer for

each process tech migration (at comparable yields). NTC is also slightly pulling in its new fab ramp,
with equipment expected to move in 1Q27. We also see a tail of DDR3 and DDR4 products NTC can
address for a while.
Nanya Tech shares trade at 2.17x NTM P/BV, above +1SD of its 10-year average. That said, we believe
the strength of the unfolding DRAM cycle, as well as the tail of legacy products that Nanya can
address as a niche player, should continue to provide support for the stock.

Our industry checks suggest HBM demand continues to result in capacity allocation away from DDR,
which may moderate possible cyclical corrections, supporting upside to DDR contract pricing for
longer.
At 2.17x NTM P/BV, we believe Nanya Tech shares are discounting a LT average ROE of 17.6% vs our
LT estimate of 21.4%.

Forecast returns
Forecast price appreciation 21.4%

Forecast stock return 27.2%
Market return assumption 6.3%
Forecast excess return 20.9%
Company Description
Nanya Tech is a pure DRAM company based in Taiwan, focusing on legacy products for
consumers, broad-based applications and is now growing in servers.
Valuation Method and Risk Statement
Nanya Tech is a pure-play DRAM manufacturer based in Taiwan mainly focused on legacy DDR

products for customers across various end applications. We think downside risks to our thesis
could include a potential slowdown in end demand and hence the conventional memory
cycle as well as Nanya's legacy-centric product portfolio. We value Nanya Tech shares using a
target NTM P/BV multiple based on long-term average ROE and cost of equity.

Quantitative Research Review
UBS Global Research publishes a quantitative assessment of its analysts' responses to certain questions about the likelihood of an occurrence
of a number of short term factors in a product known as the 'Quantitative Research Review'.  The views for this month can be found below.
Views contained in this assessment on a particular stock reflect only the views on those short term factors which are a different timeframe to

the 12-month timeframe reflected in any equity rating set out in this note.  For previous responses please make reference to (i) previous UBS
Global Research reports; and (ii) where no applicable research report was published that month, the Quantitative Research Review which can
be found at https://neo.ubs.com/quantitative, or contact your UBS sales representative for access to the report or the Quantitative Research
Team on ubs-quant-answers@ubs.com. A consolidated report which contains all responses is also available and again you should contact your

UBS sales representative for details and pricing or the Quantitative Research Team on the email above.

KEY DEFINITIONS:Forecast Stock Return (FSR) is defined as expected percentage price appreciation plus gross dividend yield over
the next 12 months. In some cases, this yield may be based on accrued dividends. Market Return Assumption (MRA) is defined as
the one-year local market interest rate plus 5% (a proxy for, and not a forecast of, the equity risk premium). Under Review (UR)
Stocks may be flagged as UR by the analyst, indicating that the stock's price target and/or rating are subject to possible change in the

near term, usually in response to an event that may affect the investment case or valuation. Equity Price Targets have an investment
horizon of 12 months.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read

the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any

issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is
not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment
to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and,

if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured
investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ
from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation

before investing in any securities.

(ESG) factors into investment process and portfolio construction. Strategies across geographies approach ESG analysis and incorporate the findings in a variety of ways.
Incorporating ESG factors or sustainable investing considerations may inhibit the portfolio manager’s ability to participate in certain investment opportunities that otherwise

would be consistent with its investment objective and other principal investment strategies. The returns on a portfolio incorporating ESG factors or sustainable investing
considerations may be lower or higher than portfolios where ESG factors, exclusions, or other sustainability issues are not considered by the portfolio manager, and the
investment opportunities available to such portfolios may differ.

Document 135: 261016_daiwa_ Ardentec

Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: We preview Ardentec’s 3Q26 results. In our initiation report, 
“Initiation: an emerging force in AI/HPC testing” (Sep 9), we expected 
traditional seasonality in 4Q26-1Q27 to weigh on Ardentec’s capacity 
utilisation. However, we now expect stronger-than-expected momentum in 
the networking segment to drive counter-seasonal resilience through 
1Q27E. We also expect AI revenue (with AI connectivity) contribution to 
exceed 40% in 1Q27E, well ahead of our original expectations.

What’s the impact: Robust networking demand bolsters high 
utilisation and CPO traction. Our industry checks indicate that demand 
for networking products is surging across the testing supply chain, primarily 
fuelled by the acceleration of 1.6T networking adoption. We forecast 
Ardentec’s revenue in 4Q26 and 1Q27 to beat typical seasonal slowdowns. 
As these high-speed networking programs largely follow a customer-
consignment model, their rising revenue mix should provide upside to 
overall gross margin. Concurrently, subsidiary Giga Solution is gaining 
momentum in co-packaged optics (CPO) testing, having already qualified 
and begun ramping up with leading US tier-1 players. While current 
engagement is centred on insertion 1, we see potential for customer base 
diversification and expansion into subsequent test insertions (2 and 3).

3Q26 results preview. With the Longtan fab expansion on track and 
spillover demand continuing, Ardentec 3Q26 revenue reached TWD5.34bn 
(+17.7% QoQ, +48.5% YoY), above the market consensus by 0.8% but 
slightly missing our estimate by 1.4%. With utilisation remaining high in 
3Q26, we estimate its gross margin and operating margin will reach 45.1% 
and 35.5%, respectively, above the consensus by 1.3pp and 1.9pp, 
respectively. We expect cleanroom construction of 2nd and 3rd floors will 
be finalised by year-end 2026 driven by solid domestic AI/HPC orders, with 
2nd-floor capacity likely contributing by late-1Q27.

What we recommend: We raise our 2027-28E EPS by 7% to reflect 
better-than-expected networking demand but lower our 2026E EPS by 1%. 
We reaffirm our Buy (1) rating and raise our 12M TP to TWD395 (from 
TWD340), based on an unchanged target PER of 25x on our 2027E EPS. 
While our target PER represents a premium over the OSAT/testing peer 
average, we believe this premium is justified by steady demand for high-
speed AI connectivity and the Longtan ramp-up. Our estimates indicate 
these factors could lift AI-related revenue to more than 40% of total sales 
by 1Q27. Key downside risk: delays in capacity expansion.

How we differ: Our 2027-28E EPS are 7-26% above the Bloomberg 
consensus, likely on our more aggressive utilisation rate and margin profile.

16 October 2026

3Q26 preview: AI defies seasonality

➢ Solid AI networking demand drives counter-seasonal resilience 
➢ On-track Longtan ramp and CPO upside expand advanced testing 
➢ Reaffirming our Buy (1) rating; raising our 12M TP to TWD395

Company profile 
Founded in 1999, Ardentec is a leading Taiwanese pure-play wafer-testing provider serving foundries, 
IDMs and fabless chipmakers. Its acquisitions of Giga Solution and Raytek over 2016-17 added RF 
testing and bumping capabilities. Combining automotive-grade reliability with flexible AI/HPC capacity, 
the company provides customers a secondary supplier which enhances supply-chain resilience.

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

Background: Zhen Ding Technology (ZDT), the world’s largest PCB 
manufacturer by revenue, generated revenue of TWD182.5bn in 2025. 
Historically focused on consumer electronics and smartphones, the 
company is shifting towards higher-value AI-server PCBs (HLC/iHDI), 
optical-module PCBs (mSAP), and IC substrates (ABF/BT). These 
businesses accounted for 21.6% of its revenue in 1H26, up from 11.7% in 
2025. Management targets this ratio to reach c.25% in 2026 and c.30% in 
2027. To support this shift, ZDT has 22 fabs under construction and plans 
to build nine more, primarily for mSAP/HDI, HLC, and ABF substrates.

Highlights: ZDT’s “One ZDT strategy” is gradually bearing fruit. Since 
2018, management has pursued its “One ZDT strategy” to broaden the 
product portfolio – including HLC, substrates, mSAP/HDI, rigid-flex and 
SLP – by leveraging its strengths in FPCB and consumer HDI. This 
strategy is beginning to pay off, and management expects its fast-growing 
segments, including mSAP HDI for optical transceivers, AI-server HDI and 
ABF/BT substrates, to gain momentum from this year. Management 
expects revenue from these segments to increase by c.150% YoY and 
account for c.25% of total revenue (or c.TWD50bn) in 2026, and to further 
increase by 100% YoY to TWD100bn in 2027. ZDT targets these fast-
growing segments to reach 30% of its revenue mix in 2027 and c.45-50% 
in 2030. Given current progress, the 2030 target could be reached in 2028. 
For mSAP HDI, management expects the 800G/1.6T transceiver product 
mix to shift to 7:3 in 2027, from 5:5 in 2026, and aims to become the largest 
supplier of optical-transceiver HDI in 2027. In AI-server HDI, we expect the 
2 leading ASIC vendors to gradually shift from pure HLC to thick HDI 
boards – a combination of HLC and HDI – in their next-generation designs, 
with ZDT a potential beneficiary. This could drive c.3x growth in ZDT’s 
business in 2027.

Product mix to support gross margin expansion. Margin expansion is 
increasingly driven by a favourable product mix. Gross margin reached 
22.4% in 1H26 (vs. 16.5% in 1H25). Management indicated that optical-
transceiver HDIs currently have the highest gross margin, at over 40%, 
followed by IC substrates, with ABF margins potentially reaching 50-60% 
and BT margins around 30% as a long-term target. Server HLC margins 
are around 20-30%, while HDI margins should be higher given their greater 
process complexity. With margins in traditional businesses at c.20%, ZDT 
expects growth in the newer segments to drive further margin expansion.

Valuation: The stock is trading at 36/19x PER for 2026/27E based on 
Bloomberg consensus. It’s past 3-year PER range is 10-30x.

20 October 2026

Business transformation bears fruit

➢ Key beneficiary of growth in optical transceiver & AI server HDI demand  
➢ Aggressive substrate capacity strategy paying off in current conditions   
➢ Margin expansion on fast-growing segments (HDI and substrates)

嘉澤：全球前三大 CPU Socket 供應商，伺服器已成營收主軸
全球前三大中央處理器插座CPU socket供應商。依Bishop統計在電腦暨週邊連
接器廠商中全球排名第四，不分行業別的全球連接器排名全球排名第十八。
主要客戶為AMD與Intel。產品涵蓋 CPU Socket、記憶體插槽、高速連接器及
線材，並延伸至 SOCAMM、液冷快接頭與 NPO Socket。
本研究預估，嘉澤伺服器營收由 2026 年221.14 億元增至 2027 年 328.67 億元，
年增 48.6%。成長來自板量回升、CPU新平台提高 Socket 與高速連接器價值，
以及 AI 新產品放量；PC 相關營收占比則由 2025 年 36.60%，降至 2027 年
16.5%，營運重心持續轉向伺服器。

CPU socket是甚麼
CPU 插槽的設計目的是將 CPU 處理器牢固地固定在主機板上，並在設備移動時
有助於防止潛在的損壞。嘉澤主要客戶為AMD與Intel。

針腳在CPU上，插入主
機板的孔位中。
CPU 底部是微小錫球，直
接高溫焊接在主機板上。
CPU 底部是平貼金屬觸點，
針腳做在主機板插槽上。外觀結構
可以。直接拉起拉桿即
可拔插更換。

卸。

1. 針腳在 CPU 上，輕

散熱表現優異。

內可容納更多觸點，能支援
更多通道與更高供電，技術
天花板最高。

理體積大，無法做到高

道（如 DDR5）訊號完
整性不如 LGA。

永久焊接，壞任何一個晶

板。

幾乎無法手動修復。

代理 AI 讓 CPU 工作量增加：從回覆問題，走向執行任務
原先：用戶提出問題；應用程式向模型發送提示；模型生成答案；應用程式將答
案傳回給用戶。主節點CPU負責調度、I/O和系統管理，而GPU則負責繁重的運
算任務。
現在：代理AI不再只是回答一個問題，而是將目標分解成多個步驟，決定下一步
該做什麼，調用多個模型，查詢資料庫，連接API，運行企業應用程序，檢查權
限，檢索內存，驗證輸出，然後再次循環執行整個過程。
CPU負責：編排：管理分解複雜任務的引擎。代理執行和工具呼叫：觸發 API 和
傳統企業軟體。策略與安全：對每個自主操作進行實際檢查。
隨 AI 從模型推論延伸至 Agent 執行，CPU 的角色擴及任務協調、工具操作及資
料處理。AMD 5 月官方文章已描述 CPU：GPU 由過去約 1：4–8，朝 1：1 移
動；Intel Q2 法說進一步表示，目前以顆數計已接近均衡。兩家原廠的觀察，支
持 CPU 在 AI 基礎建設中重要性提高

資料來源 :AMD官網(智能體人工智慧改變了CPU/GPU的平衡)

Meta Muse：個人代理產品化，使 CPU 執行環境的需求更具體
Muse 的投資意義在於：大型消費平台開始提供可持續執行任務的個人代理，使
CPU 沙盒需求從技術架構走向實際服務。
與AMD的關係Meta已部署數百萬顆AMD EPYC，並是Venice、Verano 的領先
客戶。因此，Meta 的運算擴張是 AMD CPU 需求的重要觀察對象。
持續在線的虛擬機（Persistent VMs）： Meta Muse 的底層採用了 Muse
Secure VM 架構。這些 VM 即使在使用者沒有主動點開 App 時，也在後台持續
運作，消耗大量的作業系統配置、記憶體管理與安全驗證資源—這些全部屬於通
用運算，只有 CPU 能做。因此當億萬名用戶的 AI 代理同時在線，CPU 的短缺
會遠比 GPU 來得更強烈。Muse 這樣的個人代理規模不斷擴大，對 CPU 的需求
勢必會增長，預計 CPU 與 GPU 的比例將從 1:1往4:1以上走 (intel電話會議)

開發NPO socket，為未來潛在商機
資料來源 :moneyDJ、利成光電科技有限公司
圖片來源：OIF 的《Co-Packaging Framework》第 10 頁、圖 2(d) 21
NPO的核心定義是：將光引擎從傳統的前面板可插拔位置，移動到更靠近交
換晶片（ASIC） 的地方，但光引擎和ASIC仍然是兩個獨立的封裝體。它們通
常被並排安裝在同一塊高性能基板（如矽中介層或有機基板）上
NPO Socket已進入客戶最後認證階段，且達可量產狀態，量產目標落在2027
年第3季，目前未納入估計，等待未來公司給更多指引。
AI資料中心在Scale-up與Scale-out兩種架構同步拉高頻寬需求，嘉澤在NPO、
既有高速銅傳輸與高功率連接方案的產品組合，將對應不同平台導入時程。

6274 台燿 
(Taiwan Union Technology Co.)

報告日期： 2026/10/08 研 究 員：方姿婷  (Lesley Fang) 
壹、投 資 建 議 
重點摘要： 
 2Q26 營收符合預期， 毛利率受 1H26 漲價提升 ， 預期 3Q26 毛利
將順利反應成本上漲與優化之產品組合 ， 毛利率預估提升 3%+ 。  
 AI 伺服器帶動高階銅箔基板需求強勁，同時驅動高階網路交換
器升級動能，銅箔基板需要更高速傳輸、訊號低損耗之下會陸
續採用 CCL M8、M9 規格。 
 2H26 低軌 S 客戶 V3 版本新型火箭搭載低軌衛星，所採用之銅
箔基板升級至 M8 等級；以及，1.6T 光模組需求量增帶動銅箔
基板需求量增。

一. 2Q26 營收與財報符合市場預期，3Q26 營收優於預期  
台燿 2Q26 營收為 143.01 億元，+42.2%QoQ、+110.93%YoY，毛利
率為 29.72% ，稅後淨利為 23.42 億元，單季稅後 EPS+7.82 元。2Q26
台燿受到 ASIC CCL 專案出貨帶動，產品組合優化，毛利率已相較
1Q26 季增 4.5% 。台燿已公告 3Q26 營收為 179.48 億元(優於市場預
期 3%)，+25.50%QoQ，毛利率可順利轉嫁且高階 CCL 出貨比重提
升，預估台燿 3Q26 毛利率為 33.21%，稅後淨利 36.72 億元，單季
稅後 EPS+12.60 元。

二. AI 伺服器帶動 CCL/PCB 產值提升，產能擴充 33% 
宏遠投顧觀察，AI 伺服器與先進封裝等需求日增，高階 CCL 市場
需求將在 2024 年~2026 年 CAGR 達 26% ， 而高階 CCL 供給端 CAGR
則是 7%，高階市場需求殷切。高階銅箔基板(包含 AI 伺服器和高
階網路交換器，自 2024 年始採用 M8 CCL 材料；2026 年 M8 材料
佔總體 CCL 市場 40%，M8 材料佔比顯著高於 2022 年、2023 年的
22%和 26%。台系銅箔基板供應鏈當中，M6~M8 等高階材料的供
應廠商為(2383)台光電為首、(6274)台燿次之。回顧 3 年來，台燿
產能擴充進程，端視泰國廠進度而定。4Q26 泰國新廠產能陸續開
出，台燿總產能為 260 萬張/月，+13%YoY。展望 2027 年，台燿泰
國廠、常熟廠初步先提升 30 萬張/月，逐步提升總新增產能達 120
萬張/月，預估 2027 年底時，總產能達 380 萬張/月，+46%YoY；預
估 2028 年底時，台燿總產能擴充至 530 萬張/月，+39.5%YoY，平
均 3 年每年產能擴充約 33% 。高階 CCL 在 AI 伺服器、高速網路交
投資建議：買進

三.  台燿高階網路 Switch 板材、低軌衛星板材、1.6T 光
模塊板材，27 年規格朝向高階型號發展 
展望 2H26，在高階 Switch 升級方面，800G Switch 採用 M8、M9 
CCL 規格，2H26 台燿將新增高階 Switch 客戶，隨著 AI 伺服器、
高階網路交換器產業發展朝向 800G、1.6T Switch 升級趨勢帶動之
下， 
銅箔基板需要更高速傳輸、訊號低損耗之下會陸續採用 CCL M8、
M9 規格。此外，2H26 開始 800G 與 1.6T 光纖收發模組(採用 M8、
M9 CCL)開始放量，AI 資料中心帶動光纖收發模組需求，400G 以
上光纖收發模組出貨量 2026 年 1 億個、2027 年 1.45 億個、2028 年
達到 1.6~1.65 億個；在中系系統廠商積極拉貨之下，1.6T 光纖收
發模組 2026/2027/2028 年估量 15~20M/55~60M/85~100M ， 1.6T 光纖
收發模組，於 2 年內呈倍數增長。低軌大型 S 客戶 V3 版本新型火
箭搭載低軌衛星 ， 所採用之銅箔基板升級至 M8 等級 ， 材料損耗要
求降低，訊號傳出完整性標準提高，對於 CCL、PCB(HDI)、玻纖
與銅箔等規格預期同步升級，推升材料成本與 PCB 產值。

四.預估 2026/2027 年稅後 EPS 38.49/66.22 元 
2026 年受惠 AI 伺服器及 ASIC 需求持續強勁的帶動下，台燿為網
通、高階網路交換器銅箔基板重要供貨廠商，將受惠 AI Server 對
於高階銅箔基板強勁的需求，因而，預估台燿 2026 年營收 618.78
億元，+108.01%YoY，毛利率 31.13%，稅後淨利 114.79 億元，稅後
EPS+38.49元 。 隨泰國廠產能釋出 ， 預估台燿 2027年營收增至 963.61
億元，+55.73%YoY，毛利率為 33.26%，稅後淨利 198.23 億元，稅
後 EPS+66.22 元。

與相關供應的零組件商機，以及攻入 AI 伺服器、AI 高階網路交換

二.2026~2027 年營運概況及展望 
1.HDI 與 M8 以上升級仍為關注焦點，2026~2028 年產能建置積極 
過往一般型伺服器用板採用 HDI 設計，強調細線路設計，CPU 尺
寸變大 Pin Count 數量增加至 2,000 只~4,000 只 Pin Count，PCB 板
面積不變之下要走向細線路化(線寬/線距)朝向更細線路設計。製
程上 HDI 經過多次壓合後，PP 在熱壓多次後，每一層熱膨脹係數
不同，導致通孔 Crack 或翹曲，製作時容易產生爆板，因此對於
PCB 板廠製作良率會是一大技術難關，而金像電、健鼎等廠商於
高階 HDI 相對具備生產優勢與競爭力，相對應台光電、台燿等高
階 CCL 供貨與升級也成為製程一大瓶頸；回顧 3 年來，台燿產能
擴充進程，端視泰國廠進度而定。4Q26 泰國新廠產能陸續開出，
台 燿 總 產 能 為 260 萬 張 /月，+13%YoY。展 望 2027 年，台 燿 泰 國 廠、
常熟廠初步先提升 30 萬張/月，逐步提升總新增產能達 120 萬張/
月 ， 預估 2027 年底時 ， 總產能達 380 萬張/月 ， +46%YoY ； 預估 2028
年底時，台燿總產能擴充至 530 萬張/月，+39.5%YoY，平均 3 年
每年產能擴充約 33%。高階 CCL 在 AI 伺服器、高速網路交換器
(高階 Switch)帶動之下，一旦，泰國廠產能擴充可望使得台燿市佔
率得以提升。2027 年隨著 Nvidia Rubin 平台將帶動 CCL M8+、M9
材料出貨攀升，AI ASIC CCL 拉貨動能呈現 50~60%高度成長，台
光電與台燿等高階 CCL 新產能釋出，產品組合持續優化。

針對 AI GPU 與 ASIC 發展進程，Google TPU 出貨量從 2026 年 400
萬顆上修至 2027 年(V8 增量)增至 800 萬顆，倍數增長。AWS ASIC 
預計 2027 年出貨量達 330~350 萬顆，增長+21%YoY 。NVIDIA 體系
GPU，Rubin 系列層數提升至 24~26L，材料端從 M8/M8+往更高規
格升級，更高層數與多層式架構，促使 PCB 板訊號走線、供電完
整性、高速訊號之阻抗匹配控制、訊號傳輸完整性，皆帶動 PCB

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• The recent tendency of some firms to bundle into prices
card-acceptance costs that had previously been surcharged
– and then some, in some cases – demonstrates a ‘cost-
plus’ approach to pricing. To the extent they can pass on
these costs through to prices, it suggests that demand was

strong enough to bear it. The RBA will take this as a signal
that demand is still outstripping supply.
• This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. Base wages
growth has been moderate for some years, though the
above-average increase in award wages this year will add
a little. Not all firms will seek to pass these cost increases
on, but some will, including because they have limited
scope to absorb the cost increase into margins.
• A cost-plus mentality has drawbacks, including that it

limits the imagination away from finding alternative,
efficiency-improving responses. It also accords with
recent academic research suggesting that firms form
expectations and set prices according to what is
happening in their own industry and experience, while
putting little weight on economy-wide inflation or central
bank credibility. This is an unpleasant message for many
consumers or anyone in an interest-sensitive sector when
supply shocks abound.

The recent banning of card surcharges has caused
considerable upset this month, even though it is a reversion
to historical and international practice. The card schemes
used to impose “no-surcharge” rules, until the RBA banned
these in 2003, and many other countries still have them. The
turnaround represents the RBA throwing in the towel on its
longstanding effort to use price incentives to get people to
choose low-cost payment methods. Surcharges no longer

made sense in a largely cashless economy. In any case, least-
cost routing puts the power to choose lower-cost methods in
the hands of the merchants who actually bear the cost.
In most cases, firms will simply roll the surcharge into the sticker
price, leaving the total cost to the consumer unchanged, just
more transparent. We also understand that some large retailers
have absorbed the cost – if they were surcharging in the first
place, which many did not. (The RBA estimated that around

16% of businesses imposed surcharges.) Inevitably, though,
some firms have lifted their prices by more than any reasonable
estimate of the merchant service fee that the surcharge was
meant to cover. And because this is Australia, of course takeaway
coffee has been the centre of consumer discontent.
Higher prices for one’s caffeine fix was unlikely to be the main
instigator of this month’s slump in consumer sentiment. Petrol
‘Cost-Plus’ costs
Forecast changes

• A full update to our forecasts was published in our October
Market Outlook  on Thursday 15 October.
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
prices are typically more salient for cost-of-living concerns, and
the before-and-after picture on the RBA announcement that week
shows that higher interest rates also contributed to the decline.
In some senses, though, the post-surcharge prices and the
RBA’s decisions are linked: businesses passing on increased

costs into their own prices – and expecting to be able to
make that increase stick – is exactly the kind of inflationary
environment the RBA is leaning against. Firms have been
subjected to an array of non-labour cost escalations, from
pandemic-era supply chain issues to electricity to fuel to
water to building supplies. And IT equipment prices, which
for decades were a reliable source of cost reductions, are
nowadays flat to up. Passing these costs on, leaving margins

intact, demonstrates that demand is not weak enough to
prevent this, and signals that firms’ inflationary expectations
might be higher than the RBA desires. The RBA will take this
as a sign that demand is still outstripping supply.
This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. While the Fair
Work Commission did hand down an above-average increase in
award wages this year and this was probably passed through
to prices in hospitality and other labour-intensive sectors, base

wages growth has been moderate for some years. Business
surveys point to non-labour cost escalation being the real issue
lately. This is why we were puzzled by the passage in the RBA
minutes this week suggesting that cost pressures reported
in business surveys might imply that the unemployment rate
consistent with inflation at target (the NAIRU) has risen. It is
a convenient belief, though, if unemployment is starting to
overshoot your forecasts, as the latest labour market data have

done compared with the RBA’s August forecasts.
Not all firms can pass on cost increases. Most small firms do
not have the market power, though coffee may be an exception
because convenience induces a kind of local market power. And
as noted above, large firms with everyday-low-price strategies
will instead try to find savings elsewhere in the supply chain.
Still, some firms will seek to pass these cost increases on.
Some observers might see this as corporate greed, but another

way to frame this strategy is simply as a ‘cost-plus’ mentality.
“Entrenched expectations that cost increases
can and should be passed on are precisely
what tight monetary policy is leaning against,
but only indirectly."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
It turns out that cost-plus pricing is especially common in
areas where regulators determine the price – water and other
utilities being examples where costs and prices have escalated
a lot in recent years.
There are limits to firms’ ability to absorb costs, too. The

failures of so many building firms a few years ago, squeezed
between post-pandemic cost escalation, delivery delays and
fixed-price contracts, were a case in point. Normally, there is
a contractual response, and it need not end in insolvency. The
episode does illustrate, however, that it is unrealistic to expect
firms never to pass cost increases through to prices.
The cost-plus mentality does have its drawbacks. It limits
the imagination away from finding responses that might save

costs elsewhere to absorb the cost hike, and in so doing, boost
efficiency. It was equally limiting in times of soft demand: in
the years before the pandemic, many firms professed a belief
that, because they couldn’t get price increases to stick, they
could not possibly accept cost increases, including in wages.
Thus, Australia ended up with wages growth too low to be
consistent with inflation at target, and the peculiar spectacle
of a central bank governor complaining that wages growth

was too low. And if labour remains cheap, why invest to
economise on it and thereby boost productivity?
A cost-plus mentality also amplifies the pass-through of
supply shocks. This is not quite the same thing as the ‘inflation
expectations’ seen in standard economic models of inflation,
but it is related. The stronger the tendency towards cost-plus
pricing, the worse the inflation response to a supply shock.
This is also true when inflation expectations are high, but the

distinction matters. The recent academic literature includes
some fascinating empirical evidence  from New Zealand that
suggests that beliefs about current and future overall inflation
have little bearing on many firms’ pricing decisions. As related
work using French data shows, firms’ pricing choices are
shaped less by economy-wide inflation developments than by
what is happening to their costs and in their industry.
That adds up to economy-wide behaviour that looks a lot

like firms (in aggregate) setting prices according to expected
inflation (in aggregate), but under the hood something
quite different is going on. If firms’ beliefs about future
economy-wide inflation do not matter much in their pricing
decisions, then neither does the credibility of a central bank’s
commitment to return inflation to target. The mechanism to
get inflation down then must be simply to weaken demand
enough to convince firms that cost-plus will not fly in the

market. This is an unpleasant message for many consumers
or anyone in an interest-sensitive sector when supply shocks
abound. It does, however, imply that if supply shocks fade
or even self-correct, so will high inflation. If and when that
happens, tight monetary policy can be unwound.
The deeper question is whether cost-plus price dynamics can
be reduced. That is more likely to be a question for regulators
than for central banks.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
bias towards an October or December decision. Williams
stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in
this cycle given known information. And Waller also showed
confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are

doing some of the FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest
rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating that
one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation
near the 2.0%yr medium-term target. However, as discussed
in this month’s Market Outlook , capacity pressures are likely
to see inflation consistently above target, rather than below,
in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity in the
housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to

remain modestly restrictive over the forecast horizon and will
also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade
account. At USD133.5bn, the September result was a whisker
inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More
impressively, throughout 2025 and 2026 year-to-date the
surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the
average of 2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth
of export growth is as impressive as the headline annual gain of

36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese
investment in neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time,
both trade and income receipts from offshore investment should
buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound
foreign investment should moderate the pace of gains against
other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the
evolution of producer prices across Asia, and the likelihood

of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent
decades continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was
one of the key drivers of declining goods prices. While
still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing
development to instead be driven by capital, technology and
energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking
production are also increasingly prioritising resilience, security
and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore more

probably future supply-chain developments will help contain
rising costs through productivity than drive sustained price
declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September
Labour Force Survey . Employment surprised to the
downside, –39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force
participation from August’s near-historic high largely offset.
The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August

was revised up.
Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless,
the take home point from the update is that the labour
market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs
below working-age population growth. We expect the
unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter
financial conditions and cost-of-living pressures restrict
activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast
peak is near at 4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the

communication challenge for the RBA. The September minutes
were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing
on materialising upside risks to inflation. But, with the
unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption
of 4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the
labour market is becoming harder to defend. Still, with a clear
focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our
near-term CPI forecasts in the October Market Outlook  – we

still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in
September, taking the annual rate up to 3.7%yr. Energy prices
were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation
was a touch above consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr).
Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months
(0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4% (3.1%yr) on a

0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport
services and a 0.3% increase in medical care services (2.7%yr
and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release
of the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures,
with the headline and core measures up 6.3%yr and 5.0%yr
respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these
developments, firms across multiple districts citing tariffs,
insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost

pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect
further price increases as contracts/hedges roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is
enough signal in these reports to warrant a follow-up hike
at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee
members believe they are near the end of the tightening cycle
and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman
both showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Downward revisions to 2027 outlook
This week we have updated our New Zealand economic
outlook for the next year, in particular reflecting the latest
developments in the energy market and key financial
variables (long-term interest rates and the exchange rate).

After last week’s marking down of our forecast for through-
year GDP growth in 2026 to 2.5%yr, this week we have
reduced our forecast of growth in 2027 by 0.3ppts to 2.7%yr.
This downward revision reflects a less positive assessment
of the outlooks for consumption and investment (including
residential investment) considering the more elevated path
assumed for energy prices and a higher near-term track for
long-term interest rates (as discussed in our updated Market

Outlook, published earlier this week).
A partial offset to weaker growth in domestic demand is
provided by a slightly more positive outlook for exports
(particularly exports of manufactured goods and tourism
inflows) because of a downward revision to our expectations
for the NZ dollar. We now expect NZD/USD to end this
year around current levels at 0.56 – 3c lower than expected
previously – before appreciating to around 0.64 over the
course of 2027 as interest rate differentials with the US

gradually close and as the USD appreciates. We think that the
low value of the NZ dollar – in trade-weighted terms now at a
17-year low – helps to explain the recent strong performance
in the previously moribund manufacturing sector, as well as
tourist inflows from key markets such as Australia.
Last week we raised our forecast for CPI inflation at year end
to 4.3%yr, largely on account of recent developments in global
energy prices (although the weak NZ dollar has compounded the

effect). This week we have slightly revised up our forecast for
inflation for the first half of 2027. But because energy prices are
assumed to decline in 2027 from what is now a higher starting
level, our 2027 year-end forecast for inflation has nudged
down 0.1ppts to 1.6%yr. Needless to say, forecasts of headline
inflation, in particular, are subject to significant revision given the
uncertain outlook for the Middle East conflict and uncertainty

about how that will be reflected in global energy prices.
Ahead of next week’s very important Q3 CPI report – which
could prove decisive – we have made no changes to our
forecast for the RBNZ’s policy settings. So, for now we
continue to think that despite the higher near-term inflation
outlook the RBNZ will stick with the strategy communicated
in the September Monetary Policy Statement, i.e. that at the
vv28 October meeting it will pause to assess the impact of this

year’s 50bps of policy tightening, before considering a further
upward adjustment in December.
Turning to this week’s data, the news from the housing market
remained decidedly downbeat. House sales fell a further 4% in
September – the seventh consecutive month of decline – to be
12% lower than a year earlier. With the stock of listings at an
11-year high, the median selling time remains elevated. And
so unsurprisingly, house prices fell for a fourth consecutive

month and are now down 1.6%yr. Prices remain especially
soft in the North Island, with prices down more than 3%yr in
Auckland and more than 4%yr in Wellington. Given upward
pressures on mortgage rates, broader strength in the economy
– including a stronger labour market – will likely be required to
reverse this trend. On that score, MBIE’s measure of online job
advertisements was little changed in September, continuing
the flat trend since the beginning of this year.

In other news, retail spending, as measured by electronic card
payments, decreased 0.4%m/m in September following an
unrevised 0.9% decline in August. This was a weaker result than
we had expected based on the recent sawtooth pattern and
our own internal data. We should treat these data with some
suspicion given that for quite a while now the Stats NZ series
seems not to have reflected the spending seen through banks’
own card spending networks, perhaps suggesting changes in the

payments arena may be leaving the official sector data behind.
Even so, unsurprisingly, with petrol prices rebounding late in
the month, spending on fuel increased 2.9%mth. However,
core spending, which excludes spending on fuel and vehicles,
fell 0.7%m/m. While spending on consumables (groceries)
increased modestly in September, this was at the expense of
spending on ‘nice to haves’. Spending on hospitality suffered a
Darren Gibbs
Senior Economist

further 2.3%mth fall following a 1.9%mth decline in August,
and spending on apparel replicated the 2.0%mth decline
reported in August. Spending on durable goods also fell
slightly in September (-0.4%mth).
Despite the weak outcome for the August and September

months, retail spending over the September quarter still
eked out a minor 0.1% gain, while core spending increased
0.4%. Given higher prices, at face value this points to a weak
quarter for volumes (next week’s CPI report will cast more
light on pricing behaviour during the quarter). It is worth
noting that total retail spending, as will be depicted in the
official quarterly retail sales survey released next month, could
point to somewhat stronger growth. Purchases of big-ticket

items that often are bought on credit – such as cars, furniture
and appliances – are underrepresented in the electronic card
spending survey.
Given subdued domestic demand, local hospitality businesses
have relied on foreign visitors to drive growth in spending.
And the good news is that visitor arrivals increased to a new
post-Covid high in August to be up 8%yr. Arrivals of Chinese
citizens have grown by almost a third to sit near pre-Covid
levels in recent months, while arrivals of US citizens grew

17%yr. However, the Middle East conflict has impacted
long-haul arrivals from Europe (UK arrivals are down 8%yr).
Meanwhile, inflows of migrants remain at modest levels,
totalling around 20,000 in the year to August – about half
what might be expected on average over the cycle.
News from the manufacturing sector remained positive in
September, with the BusinessNZ Manufacturing PMI rising
to a 3-month high of 56.5 in September. Improvement was
seen across all subcomponents of the index, with the new

orders index rising 1.8pts to 57.0. We think that the recovery in
manufacturing this year owes much to the weakness of the NZ
dollar – especially against the Australian dollar – and activity
associated with a strong domestic primary sector and an
emerging recovery in domestic residential construction activity.
Last but certainly not least, ahead of next week’s all-important
Q3 CPI, the Selected Prices report for September was firmer
than expected. That was mainly due to larger than expected

increases in airfares and holiday accommodation costs, which
offset softer than expected food prices.
Accounting for the September prices data, we’ve left our
forecast for Q3 inflation  at 0.7%. That would see the annual
inflation rate dropping back to 3.7%, down from 4.1% in the
year to June. Underlying that move has been a moderation in
fuel prices after they rocketed higher in the wake of the Middle
East war. That’s balanced against increases in council rates,

building costs and food prices.
Our forecast is lower than the RBNZ’s September MPS
forecast for a 0.8% rise (+3.9%yr). However, with some large
swings in volatile items like fuel, the RBNZ will likely focus
on measures of core inflation. Core inflation was already at
firm levels in the June quarter, with key measures sitting in
the upper part of the RBNZ’s target band. We expect that
they will take another step higher in September, to sit around

3%. Upside surprises in core inflation would be an important
development for the RBNZ ahead of the October OCR review,
as this could suggest that the lower NZD and higher oil prices
are boosting underlying price pressures by more and sooner
than they may have expected.

### Page 7

Q3 is likely to produce a very similar outcome to Q2, with 
quarterly growth of around 1.0% holding the annual rate at 
4.3%yr. Year-to-date, growth will remain around 4.7%ytd 
thanks to the Q1 result.
The detail and partial data for September will again highlight 
the weak state of the consumer and domestic-oriented 
businesses, but also the incredible strength of exports. With 
the trade surplus back at record highs, there are limits to the 
contribution possible from net exports hence, necessitating 
a strengthening of domestic demand if aggregate growth is 
to continue at the rate authorities intend into 2027. Expect 
additional stimulus through the turn of the year.

澜起科技 2026 年三季度预计营收约 20.79 亿元、同⽐增⻓约 46% ，归⺟净利润 12 亿⾄ 13 亿
元、同⽐增⻓超 150% ，盈利增速显著⾼于收⼊增速。增⻓主要受益于互连类芯⽚出货增加
及 DDR5 ⾼代际 RCD 芯⽚占⽐提升，⽑利率升⾄ 68% 以上。公司预计四季度相关产品需求
进⼀步提升，业绩仍有望延续增⻓。
摘要：
澜起科技三季度营收和利润均实现较快增⻓，盈利增速显著⾼于收⼊增速。公司预计， AI 推理及
Agent 应⽤发展带动服务器和内存需求增⻓， DDR5 ⾼代际产品出货占⽐提升，推动业绩进⼀步
改善。
10 ⽉ 20 ⽇，澜起科技披露 2026 年第三季度业绩预增公告，预计三季度实现营业收⼊约 20.79 亿
元，同⽐增⻓约 46.0% ，环⽐增⻓约 10.9% 。
利润⽅⾯，公司预计三季度归⺟净利润为 12 亿⾄ 13 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 153.6% ⾄ 174.7% ；扣⾮归
⺟净利润为 9.5 亿⾄ 10.5 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 152.6% ⾄ 179.2% ，环⽐增⻓ 32.2% ⾄ 46.1% 。

业绩增⻓主要受益于互连类芯⽚出货增加及产品结构改善。公司同时预计，第四季度相关产品
需求将进⼀步提升，后续业绩表现仍需关注需求兑现情况。

DDR5 ⾼代际产品出货增⻓，⽑利率稳步提升
澜起科技预计，三季度互连类芯⽚产品线销售收⼊约 20.47 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 49.3% ，环⽐增⻓
20.9% ，占公司营业收⼊约 98.5% ，是本季度收⼊增⻓的主要来源。
产品结构⽅⾯， DDR5 第三、第四、第五⼦代 RCD （寄存器时钟驱动器）芯⽚出货占⽐进⼀步提
升。随着内存接⼝芯⽚向更⾼代际迭代，产品结构优化也为⽑利率改善提供了⽀撑。
公司预计，三季度互连类芯⽚产品线⽑利率为 68.0% ⾄ 68.5% ，同⽐提升 2.31 ⾄ 2.81 个百分点，
环⽐提升 0.57 ⾄ 1.07 个百分点。
⽑利率持续上⾏，意味着本季度利润增⻓不仅来⾃出货量增加，也受益于产品结构改善。营收
同⽐增⻓约 46% ，⽽归⺟净利润同⽐增⻓超过 150% ，反映出收⼊增⻓与利润率提升共同放⼤了

盈利弹性。
AI 推理需求带动内存互连芯⽚增⻓，四季度需求值得关注
随着 AI 推理及 Agent 应⽤发展，服务器对内存容量和带宽的需求持续增加， DDR5 内存及配套互
连芯⽚有望继续受益。澜起科技作为内存接⼝芯⽚供应商，主要通过相关芯⽚出货增⻓及产品
迭代获得增量。
对于后续需求，公司在公告中预计，第四季度相关产品需求将进⼀步提升。这为短期业绩延续
增⻓提供了⽀撑，但实际表现仍取决于服务器需求、产品出货及产品结构变化。
若需求按预期增⻓，叠加⾼代际产品占⽐继续提升，澜起科技的收⼊和利润仍有进⼀步增⻓的
空间。

Document 311: GF - PTFE Adoption

Source PDF: all report pdf/GF - PTFE Adoption.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

Equity Research 
Sector Report | Overseas Technology｜Oct 15,2026 
PTFE Adoption: 
Reshape the High-End Materials Landscape

Overseas  
Technology

Michelle Jing 
SFC CE No. BUK594 
michellejing@gfgroup.com.hk 
Jeff Pu, CFA 
SFC CE No. BNO719 
jeffpu@gfgroup.com.hk

What's new 
Nvidia showcased its NVLink PTFE board prototype for the first time at OCP Global Summit 2026, highlighting seven supply-chain partners: 
Taiflex (8039 TT), Flexium (6269 TT), TTM Technologies (TTMI US), 3 leading Chinese PCB manufacturers, and 1 leading Chinese CCL 
supplier. We believe this development reinforces our previous view that PTFE remains the most promising material solution for the VR300 
NVL576 switch board, offering the best balance between electrical performance and MP feasibility. 
Comments 
PTFE hybrid remains the preferred solution: As highlighted in our Oct. 5 report, we continue to favor the PTFE hybrid solution for the 
VR300 NVL576 switch board among the various options, given its optimal balance between electrical performance and MP feasibility. 
While pure PTFE remains challenging to process, particularly in lamination and drilling, leading PCB manufacturers have achie ved 
encouraging yields with hybrid structures that combine PTFE signal layers with M9 materials in non-signal layers. 
We like PTFE's long-term adoption potential: PTFE applications in high-frequency and high-speed interconnects could extend beyond 
switch boards. The IEC has published a standard on PTFE-filled CCL, led by the leading Chinese CCL company, highlighting the material's 
relevance to broader circuit-board applications. Our checks also suggest that PTFE materials have entered the testing phase for other 
applications, including compute boards. We therefore remain positive on PTFE's long-term adoption across a broader range of high-end 
PCB applications. 
Limited near-term impact on conventional resin materials, but long-term substitution risks warrant attention: Under the current 
PTFE hybrid design, we expect 8–12 out of 32 layers to adopt PTFE for signal transmission, accounting for less than 50% of the total CCL 
resin content value. Over the longer term, we expect a potential shift toward pure PTFE solutions as improvements in processing 
technology enable commercially viable MP yields, reducing the need for hybrid structures. This could gradually increase substitution risks 
for conventional high-end PCB resin materials, including PPO and HC resin. 
Favor PTFE-exposed suppliers with proven technical and manufacturing capabilities: We remain positive on PTFE-related 
opportunities, particularly suppliers with strong R&D capabilities, proven MP expertise, and established customer qualifications. In CCL, 
we favor Taiflex (8039 TT) and leading Chinese CCL supplier for their PTFE material capabilities and supply-chain exposure. Among the 
five Nvidia-announced PCB suppliers, we favor the leading Chinese PTFE PCB supplier, given its advanced hybrid PTFE processing 
capabilities and encouraging yields. For CCL suppliers that have yet to enter the PTFE supply chain, we recommend monitoring progress 
in developing, while remaining alert to potential market-share losses in their conventional product portfolios. 
Risks  
1) AI demand slowing down; 2) Geo-political risks; 3) Competition.

Document 345: Persistent energy pressures have further to run (2026-10-21)

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
BULLETIN
Persistent energy pressures have further to run
19 October 2026
Key points
• Shipping disruptions, depleted
inventories and elevated freight
costs are keeping physical crude
and fuel markets tight despite a

reported recovery in Gulf exports.
• Brent is forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing
to US$77/bbl in Q4 2027.
• Refined product markets are
expected to ease more slowly than
crude, with elevated gasoil cracks,
shipping and insurance costs slowing
declines in petrol and diesel prices.
• Under an escalation scenario,
renewed conflict and deeper supply
disruptions could push Brent towards
US$150/bbl in H1 2027, prolonging
elevated fuel and LNG prices.

• Under an early resolution scenario, a
durable US-Iran agreement accelerates
supply recovery, taking Brent towards
US$65/bbl by Q4 2027 and bringing
fuel and LNG prices down faster.
Q4 2026 forecasts:
Brent crude
US$101/bbl
Japanese LNG
US$24.5/MMBtu

### Page 2

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should

ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could
push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
This article was first published in the October Market Outlook .
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating

global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite reported crude exports through the Strait of
Hormuz recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels
in late September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back to
normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings have
risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving visible

transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo transfers in
the Gulf of Oman have increased as an alternative route.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly. Ship-
to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts through
the Gulf via a route close to the Oman shoreline cause delays
while some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships are
also available, with many redeployed to other markets. Higher
insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-China

tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher than a
year ago. With prompt barrels (available for physical delivery)
scarce, the gap between physical and futures prices widened
again, with Dated Brent around US$136/bbl in early October
against US$101/bbl for front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes

on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the
Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened
to an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October,
from US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the Australian
government’s excise tax returning to normal, after it had been

halved, this has lifted national diesel and petrol prices to
around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a
return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average

around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around
US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of

this reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist

### Page 3

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
attacks on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below
its pre-conflict level until H2 2027. Meanwhile, we look for UAE
production to continue to improve over 2027 and lift above
pre-conflict levels from 2028, as it is no longer constrained by

OPEC quotas. We do not expect a meaningful return of Iranian
oil until an agreement is reached, and damage sustained during
the conflict means production will take time to recover.
We expect global oil demand to be 103.7mb/d in 2026, 0.8mb/d
(0.7%) higher than in 2025, and to rise a further 1.9mb/d (1.8%)
to 105.6mb/d in 2027. Chinese crude imports will continue to
recover over the coming months as refiners restock ahead of
Lunar New Year, but we expect only a gradual return to pre-

conflict levels, as the electrification of transport limits the
recovery in Chinese demand. Globally, demand for diesel and jet
fuel recovers as transport and aviation activity picks up, although
some demand destruction and efficiency gains are expected
to be permanent. The conflict has also highlighted strategic
vulnerabilities, particularly across Asia, so we expect a push to
rebuild reserves adding around 0.5% to demand over 2027.
Fuel prices to ease more slowly than crude

Refined product supply is expected to recover more slowly
than crude, with Gulf refinery runs not returning to pre-war
levels until at least late 2027. Outside the Gulf, there has
been some constructive news. The US has lifted sanctions
on Russian diesel until April 2027, with Russia saying it will
supply more than 4Mt over the coming months, while China
has lifted its ban on refined product exports. However, we
are cautious about how much this will add to the market in

the short term. Much of the Russian supply is headed to the
US and will depend on Russia’s ability to restore refining and
export capacity, while doubts remain over the Russia-Ukraine
energy ceasefire announced by President Trump. China still
manages exports through quotas, which are likely to increase
only gradually as domestic stocks are rebuilt. Refinery runs
should lift ahead of Lunar New Year, but any increase in
exports will take time to reach regional markets.

With product supply recovering slowly and depleted inventories
being rebuilt, Singapore gasoil crack is likely to stay above its
long-run average even as Brent eases, narrowing from US$71/
bbl in Q4 to US$27/bbl by Q4 2027. With Singapore Gas 10ppm
prices, shipping and insurance costs to remain elevated and
easing only gradually in 2027, we forecast Australian diesel
to retail at an average of 274¢/L in Q4, easing to 200¢/L in Q4
2027, and petrol at 230¢/L and 187¢/L respectively.

LNG prices supported through 2027
Japanese LNG prices surged 15.2%mth in September,
averaging US$25.4/MMBtu as supply disruptions in Qatar
and its inability to divert exports around the Strait of Hormuz
tightened markets and intensified competition between
importers. While prices are now more than double pre-conflict
levels, demand destruction across Asia is providing some
offset to tight supply
Our Q4 forecast for Japanese LNG is now US$24.5/mmbtu and

we expect the market to remain structurally tight, with supply
recovery likely to be gradual and prices not returning to pre-
conflict levels until around 2028. Still, Australia’s domestic
gas market should remain insulated, supported by the FY2027
Federal Budget’s domestic gas reservation scheme, which
requires exporters to supply the domestic market with the
equivalent of up to 20% of gas exports. So far this year,
despite the increase in LNG spot prices in Asia, Australian

natural gas prices have remained under the $12 per GJ cap
that has proposed by the Commonwealth government. We are
expecting this to continue well into 2027.
Risks around our oil price outlook are two-sided
Talks are still ongoing in the background, with the US and
Iran exchanging proposals to reopen the Strait, but the gap
between them remains wide. The conflict has also expanded
to the Red Sea and, with inventories sharply drawn down,
there is a smaller buffer to absorb a further shock.

Using the Oxford Economics model, we assess an escalation
scenario, where energy markets face a deeper and more
persistent disruption as the conflict in the Middle East
intensifies. In this case, Brent futures rise to around US$150/
bbl in H1 2027, only dipping to below US$100/bbl in Q2 2028.
We also consider an early resolution scenario, where shipping
and Gulf supply normalise faster, returning Brent to near pre-
conflict levels by end-2027.
Escalation scenario

In the escalation scenario we assume:
Talks stall and the conflict escalates:  the gap between the
US and Iran proves too wide and the US resumes strikes after
the midterms. Iran responds with attacks on shipping and Gulf
energy infrastructure, damaging export, storage and refining
facilities. A lasting agreement is not reached until 2028.
Export routes are disrupted: more frequent attacks curtail
dark crossings of the Strait and ship-to-ship transfers in the

Gulf of Oman. Houthi attacks also cut off Saudi alternative
routes, forcing Gulf producers to cut output and Middle
East crude exports sharply lower. New routes and shipping
arrangements eventually support a recovery in Gulf flows, but
trade patterns take time to adjust.
... and risks remain and while they are two sided ...

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Product supply fails to recover:  The Russia–Ukraine energy
ceasefire does not hold and strikes on Russian refineries
continue, limiting how much diesel Russia can export,
even with the US sanctions waiver extended. China also

reintroduces its export ban to protect domestic supply.
Oil prices lift to around US$150/bbl in H1 2027: With little
buffer to absorb the supply loss, competition for physical
delivery intensifies, with physical prices well above futures
as prompt barrels remain scarce. Higher prices support
supply growth outside OPEC and drive demand destruction,
alongside further emergency stock releases, but a deeper
and more prolonged global deficit remains. Brent stays above

US$100/bbl until mid-2028 as governments rebuild reserves.
The Singapore gasoil crack widens and, with freight and
insurance costs also higher, Australian diesel and petrol prices
average around 247 cpl and 231 cpl at end 2027. Japanese
LNG also remains elevated around US$23.2/MMBtu in Q4
2027, $9.1 above our baseline.
Early resolution scenario
In the early resolution scenario we assume:
A durable agreement: The US and Iran agree terms to reopen
the Strait by the end of year, and, unlike the June interim deal,

the agreement holds, with progress towards a permanent
settlement in early 2027. Attacks on shipping cease and the
US blockade is lifted, allowing Iranian oil back into the market,
initially from storage. Vessels are quickly repositioned to the
Gulf, with freight and war-risk insurance easing from early
2027 rather than mid-year.
A faster recovery in supply:  With export routes secure, Gulf
production and damaged refinery and export capacity recover

sooner than in the baseline. Outside the Gulf, the US extends
its waiver on Russian diesel beyond April 2027 and China
raises export quotas as domestic stocks are rebuilt.
Demand settles below pre-conflict trend: Some demand
destruction and efficiency gains persist, particularly in China.
With inventories rebuilding faster, government restocking is
smaller and more gradual than in the baseline.
Prices fall quickly, but restocking provides a floor:  Brent

Brent futures ease to around US$88/bbl in Q1 2027 as the
premium for prompt physical barrels unwinds. As the market
moves into balance, Brent eases to around US$65/bbl by Q4
2027, close to pre-conflict levels.
Refined product prices ease faster on lower freight costs with
the Singapore gasoil crack narrowing. Australian diesel and
petrol prices fall to around 182 cpl and 170 cpl by Q4 2027. Gas
markets also ease, although damage to Qatar’s gas facility means

export capacity recovers gradually. Japanese LNG falls to around
US$11.9/MMBtu by end-2027, $2.1 below our baseline.
Economic impact
In the escalation scenario, higher fuel costs flow through to
transport, food and other energy-intensive goods such as
plastics and fertilisers. Australian headline CPI remains above
4.5% for much of 2027, with underlying inflation remaining
sticky at above 3% until late 2028. The RBA faces a difficult
trade off between much higher inflation and a significant

hit to activity, as households cut discretionary spending and
businesses defer investment. Higher LNG export earnings
provide only a moderate offset. In the early resolution scenario,
headline inflation is around 0.1ppt below the baseline in 2027.
New Zealand is more exposed in both scenarios, with food
and energy a larger share of its CPI and no offsetting energy
exports. Headline inflation is around 1ppt higher in the
escalation scenario in Q1 2027.

... the upside presents a more dramatic shift in prices

Document 346: Poland’s K-shaped recovery weighs on growth, supports stable rates

Source PDF: all report pdf/Poland’s K-shaped recovery weighs on growth, supports stable rates.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期

### Page 1

September’s data highlights Poland’s K-shaped economy:
resilient industry contrasts slumping construction and
weakness in sectors exposed to Asian competition. Hopes for
an investment rebound are fading, while wage pressures
remain moderate despite higher inﬂation. Producer price
increases remain largely energy-led, with limited spillover

Leszek Kasek and Mateusz Sutowicz
Warsaw, Poland. The country's K-shaped story is bad news for the economy,
but good news for inﬂation and signals no change to National Bank of Poland
rates this year
Higher inﬂation has a limited impact on
wage growth.
Average wages in Poland rose by 5.9% year-on-year in September,
up from 5.6% in August; both our forecast and consensus expected
6.0%. Volatile sectors drove the acceleration: agriculture, forestry,
hunting and ﬁshing rebounded to 6.8% from -11.9%, while mining
and quarrying rose to 1.8% from -1.7%.

Underlying wage pressures are better reﬂected in services, where
growth remained broadly stable. Accommodation and food
services recorded 5.6% YoY versus 5.1% in August, transportation
and storage 5.9% versus 6.0%, and information and
communication 6.6% versus 7.1%.
Moderate services wage pressures limit the risk of entrenched
services inﬂation and support gradual core disinﬂation.
Manufacturing wage growth was also stable at 5.6% YoY, versus
5.5% in August.

Continued decline in corporate sector
employment
Average employment fell by 0.8% YoY in September, matching both
our forecast and consensus. Jobs declined by 7k from August to
6,360k and by almost 50k over 12 months. Most major sectors
recorded declines, led by mining and quarrying (-9.1%) and
professional, scientiﬁc and technical activities (-2.1%).
Accommodation and food services grew by 2.0%.
The persistent decline partly reﬂects retirements not being fully

oﬀset by new labour market entrants. However, corporate sector
data mainly covers ﬁrms employing at least 10 people. Changing
employment arrangements and the growing role of foreign workers
complicate interpretation, with these ﬁgures showing steeper
declines than economy-wide data.
September’s labour market data conﬁrms gradually easing wage
pressures, with no clear inﬂation pass-through despite elevated and
partly energy-driven price growth.
Construction slumps in September

Construction output fell by 9% YoY, versus August’s 6.6% growth,
our 2.7% forecast and the 3.4% consensus. Following August’s
National Recovery Plan (NRP) grant milestone deadline, civil
engineering fell by 4.1%, after 29% growth. Specialised
construction declined by 2.7%, versus 3.1% growth. Buildings and
structures fell by 19.2%, following a 19.5% decline in August, as

Producer price inﬂation picked up
signiﬁcantly but remains conﬁned to
energy-related categories
Producer price inﬂation accelerated to 6.1% YoY from 4.8% in
August, above our forecast and consensus of 5.7%, driven by
global commodity prices.
Coke and reﬁned petroleum products dominated price increases.

Higher copper and coal prices lifted mining prices by 7.8% YoY,
versus 6.8% in August. Despite rising energy costs, other categories
show little pressure, suggesting limited spillovers beyond energy-
related prices.
The bottom line? Today's data supports no
change in 2026 NBP policy rates
Overall, September’s data reinforces Poland’s K-shaped recovery: a
resilient industry contrasts with weak construction, tempering the
growth outlook. Moderate wage pressures and limited spillovers

from energy-led producer inﬂation favour gradual core disinﬂation.
Taken together, the readings support our forecast of unchanged
National Bank of Poland interest rates in 2026.

SUMMARY: AI euphoria meets macro headwinds
Tech remained a major driver of equity market performance in Q3. The
Mag 7 outperformed the broader market while the S&P 500 also

posted gains, lifted by mega-cap and tech stocks.
Still, tech’s path in Q3 was volatile. July began with a reversal of the AI
trade as investors questioned elevated valuations, the sustainability of
AI spending and the outlook for semiconductor demand. That led to a
large sell-off in chip stocks, with large falls in Q2 outperformers SK
Hynix and Samsung contributing to a sharp decline in Korea’s KOSPI.
The Philly SOX experienced its worst monthly performance since

2008.
By August, however, sentiment had improved. The S&P 500 reached
fresh record highs, even as US Treasury yields rose, supported by
stronger cloud earnings and signs of AI monetization. September
proved more challenging as higher oil prices, inflation concerns and
rising bond yields weighed on risk assets, particularly semiconductors.
The Fed also raised its policy rate by 25bp on Sep 16.
Yet, despite these macro headwinds, company guidance continued to

indicate strong AI infrastructure demand, with Micron’s stronger-than-
expected revenue guidance on Sep 30 providing further evidence of
robust demand for memory chips. Memory supply remains tight,
semiconductor capex is accelerating, and the industry’s attention has
increasingly turned to bottlenecks in optical networking and photonics,
as well as concerns around AI regulation and safety. Looking ahead,
tech is likely to remain an important driver of equity returns into Q4,

although elevated valuations and tighter financial conditions leave the
sector sensitive to earnings disappointments. Sources: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Updated Sep 30. Returns in local currency.

1. GLOBAL INDUSTRY BREAKDOWN
Tech hardware, including Lenovo, Advantech, and HP Inc, rebounded in Q3. This was partly driven by accelerating AI

server demand and the ongoing data center build-out, although continuing memory shortages raised costs for
hardware manufacturers. Cloud and server providers, like Oracle, Dell and HPE, also highlighted strong AI-related
demand. Semis and equipment were the weakest performers. Elsewhere, entertainment and telecoms posted modest
declines. Finally, in Q3 investor attention increasingly shifted to AI agents, with Meta’s Muse, Microsoft’s Copilot and

Salesforce’s Agentforce among the prominent offerings.

2. SEMICONDUCTORS
Chip stocks were hurt initially by a reversal of the AI trade and then by rising bond yields. The Philly SOX index fell
sharply in Q3, after its worst monthly performance in July (-21%) since 2008. Concerns about higher oil prices and
rising rates continued to weigh on the sector in Aug and Sep. SK Hynix was among the weakest performers, amid an

earnings disappointment relative to expectations and an unwinding of leveraged positions. Its Nasdaq ADRs began
trading in July. Despite these headwinds, the semis outlook remains supported by AI infrastructure demand. Within
semis, potential long-term beneficiaries include suppliers of AI infrastructure, memory including high-bandwidth
memory (HBM), advanced packaging, photonics and semiconductor equipment.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Updated Sep 30. Returns in local currency.

4. REGIONAL BREAKDOWN
After outperforming in Q2, mainland Chinese tech stocks posted large declines this quarter, hurt by July’s sell-off.
The CSI 300 tech series led the declines. Weakness also extended across Asia more broadly, with the KOSPI, Nikkei 225
and Hang Seng Tech down in Q3, driven by a chip sell-off and concerns over further Fed hikes. European tech equities
were more resilient, with the STOXX Europe 600 Tech Index down slightly lower in Q3 but still well above its year-end

level. Within European tech, Infineon underperformed, while Orange illustrated weakness in telecoms outside the index.

Document 392: United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24)

### Page 1

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
A SHOCK-RESISTANT EXPANSION
US growth remains resilient despite successive shocks. According to our forecasts, it is expected to reach 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027. Its main driver is still investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has remained entirely unaffected by the
energy crisis. The labour market has seen a marked improvement in job creation and a fall in the unemployment rate. Core

inflation remains stubbornly high and above the Federal Reserve (Fed)’s target. Against this backdrop, the Fed has begun a
hiking cycle in September, which, according to our scenario, would bring the Fed Funds target to 4.50% (upper limit) by Q1
2027. At the same time, long-term rates are under upward pressure.
1  This includes: energy and communications; data centres (facilities); IT equipment and peripherals; communication equipment (equipment); software; and research and develop-

ment (intellectual-property products).
THE ENERGY SHOCK IS NOT HOLDING BACK GROWTH
Driven by AI-related investment, US growth is holding up despite ri -
sing energy inflation. We forecast GDP growth at 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027 on an annual average basis. The AI-related investment
cycle1 (+14.8% y/y in H1 2026) is expected to continue, accompanied by
potential productivity gains. However, other private-investment seg -
ments (including residential property) remain sluggish. In addition, the

rising cost of capital could accentuate its ‘K-shaped’ profile. Household
consumption is expected, on the whole, to benefit from the rebound in
job creation and to remain above 2% y/y through to the end of 2027.
In H1 2026, it held up against resurging inflation due to an average
year-on-year increase of 11.5% in tax refunds and a savings rate stan-
ding at its lowest level since 2022 (2.8% in Q2).
RECORD CAPITAL-RAISING DRIVEN BY AI
This year, AI-related investments are driving massive capital-raising

by companies through loans, bond issues or share placements. This
trend is set to continue given the seemingly colossal scale of the fun -
ding requirements, even as risk premia in the bond market begin to
widen. According to their statements, the five tech giants could invest
nearly USD 900 bn this year and over USD 1,000 bn next year—more
than double the figure for 2025. Spurred on by regulatory easing, the
major US banks have announced that they will mobilise significant re-

sources to support projects considered strategic for the United States
(AI, defence, energy and infrastructure). While funding for the AI boom
appears unaffected by rising long-term interest rates, these latter are
likely to continue to weigh on household demand for mortgages. Since
the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 30-year mortgage rates
have risen by 78 bp (to 6.76% at the start of September). Conversely,
consumer lending remained buoyant in H1 2026 and is supporting

household spending.
TWO-SPEED GROWTH IN INDUSTRY
Industrial production rebounded slightly in 2025 (+1.1%), driven by AI
and aerospace. We forecast an acceleration to +1.5% in 2026 and +2%
in 2027. Investment in AI is bolstering the electronics sector, while the
(civil and military) aerospace sector is rebounding as order backlogs
are cleared and defence requirements rise. Conversely, sectors ex -
posed to rising tariffs (in particular, the steel, aluminium and auto -

motive sectors) are still on the back burner, contrary to the objective
of these measures. This positive momentum in industrial production,
while concentrated, is expected to continue, bolstered by the recove -
ry in business sentiment. The ISM Manufacturing Index has been in
expansionary territory since early 2026, despite ongoing pressures on
prices and supply chains.
